text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"python -X utf8 -c \"import json,pathlib;d=json.load(open('audits/skills-2026-10-09/global-review.json',encoding='utf-8'));names=['firecrawl-developer-index','firecrawl-research-index'];[(print('\\nFILE',n,'\\n',pathlib.Path(next(x['path'] for x in d['rows'] if x['name']==n)).read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig'))) for n in names]\"",max_output_tokens:6000}));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"4fef33","wall_time_seconds":0.3983111,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":4421,"output":"\\r\\nFILE firecrawl-developer-index \\r\\n ---\\r\\nname: firecrawl-developer-index\\r\\ndescription: Search issues, merged pull requests, READMEs, and documentation. Use when the question is how a library or API behaves, what an error means, or whether a bug was fixed; prefer this over a general web page.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Firecrawl Developer Index\\r\\n\\r\\nAnswer a developer question from the primary source: the issue where the bug was reported, the merged pull request that fixed it, the README or documentation page that states the contract. A blog post that describes a behaviour is a weaker answer than the passage that defines it, so reach for the index first and the open web second.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere is **no fixed recipe**. Read the question, decide what kind it is, and choose the approach below. A literal error string wants a different move than \\"how do I do X\\". Don\'t run machinery a question doesn\'t call for.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The tools, and what each is uniquely good at\\r\\n\\r\\n- HTTP: **`GET|POST https://api.firecrawl.dev/v2/search/developer`**\\r\\n  MCP: **`firecrawl_developer_search(query, k?, skills?)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl developer <query> [--limit <n>]`**\\r\\n  Ranked results over the whole index. Each carries `id` (`issue:owner/repo#123`), `url`, and the **matched passages in markdown**, so tables and code blocks survive. The artifact kind is the `id` prefix: `doc:`, `issue:`, `pull_request:`, or `readme:`.\\r\\n  The default first move for a developer question. It is the only surface that returns the passages, which is what lets you answer instead of pointing at a page.\\r\\n  `k` / `--limit` is 1–100 and defaults to 10. `skills=\\"only\\"` (HTTP/MCP only) restricts the search to agent-skill files.\\r\\n  Keyless; send `Authorization: [REDACTED] $FIRECRAWL_API_KEY` for higher rate limits.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_search(query, categories: [\\"developer\\"])`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl search <query> --categories developer`**\\r\\n  Developer hits in a `developer` group beside `web`, each with `url`, `title`, `description` (the matched passage), `position`, and `category: \\"developer\\"` — web results carry no `category`, so that is the field to key on when merging.\\r\\n  Use this when you are **already** running a web search and want developer sources weighed in the same call. It exposes none of the filters and no passage control.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_scrape(url)` / `firecrawl_search(query)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl scrape <url>` / `firecrawl search <query>`**\\r\\n  General web fetch and search, for what no primary source states: a comparison between two libraries, an outage, a migration write-up, a project with no public repository or indexed docs.\\r\\n  Also the follow-through when a hit is the right page but you need all of it — `scrape` the result\'s `url`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Filters, and what each one costs you\\r\\n\\r\\nOnly the HTTP surface takes these. On `GET`, pass `types=issue,pull_request` or repeat the parameter; on `POST`, pass arrays. All are optional.\\r\\n\\r\\n- `types` — which of `doc`, `issue`, `pull_request`, `readme` to search. Defaults to all four. Narrowing here is the cheapest way to sharpen a query.\\r\\n- `repos` (`owner/name`) scopes the repository half, meaning `issue`, `pull_request`, and `readme`; `sources` (documentation source ids, at most 20) scopes the documentation half, meaning `doc`. Passing both **unions** the halves rather than intersecting them. Both echo back in the response with `indexed: true|false` — that is how you tell \\"not in the index\\" from \\"found nothing\\".\\r\\n- A filter that cannot match any requested `type` is a `400`, not an empty list: `repos` with no repository type in `types`, or `sources` without `doc`.\\r\\n- `passages` (1–5, default 1) is the _maximum_ passages per result, not a guarantee. Raise it when one page is clearly the right page but the first passage is the wrong part of it.\\r\\n- `language`, `topic`, `license`, `min_stars`, `max_stars`, `archived`, `fork` describe a **repository**. Most documentation pages in the index have no repository behind them, so no repository fact can admit or exclude one. Send any of these without a `sources` scope and the response holds repository evidence only — `issue`, `pull_request`, `readme`. That is the design, not an index fault: do not retry it and do not report the index broken. To keep documentation, drop the repository filters, or scope the documentation half with `sources` and read the `sources` echo to confirm the id is indexed.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Match the approach to the question\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Literal error message or stack-trace string** → search the string itself plus the library name, with `types=[\\"issue\\",\\"pull_request\\"]`. Whoever hit it filed it. If nothing matches, strip the volatile parts (paths, line numbers, ids, addresses) and retry — the invariant middle of the message is what is indexed.\\r\\n- **Conceptual \\"how do I do X\\"** → the full question in natural language, all four types. The answer is usually a `doc` or a `readme`; raise `passages` before raising `k`.\\r\\n- **Known bug** → the issue reports it, the merged pull request _fixes_ it, and the fix is what you want. Search `types=[\\"issue\\",\\"pull_request\\"]`, then re-query the issue\'s own terms scoped to its repo with `types=[\\"pull_request\\"]`. A merged PR\'s passages tell you what changed and in which direction.\\r\\n- **API contract** (\\"what does X return\\", \\"is Y required\\", \\"what is the default\\") → `readme` and `doc` are authoritative and a blog post is not. Use `types=[\\"readme\\",\\"doc\\"]`. If the contract looks like it moved, follow up with `pull_request` for the change that moved it.\\r\\n- **Version-specific behaviour** → an issue\'s opening report describes the broken version; its resolution supersedes it. Raise `passages` to see further into the thread, and read the resolution and the linked pull request before answering. Never answer from an opening report alone.\\r\\n- **Scoped to one library** → `repos=[\\"owner/name\\"]` when you know the slug, plus `sources` if you want its docs in the same call. If a scoped search comes back empty, read the echoed `indexed` flag first: `false` means nothing from that repo or source can ever match and no rephrasing will help — drop the scope and search the whole index, or go to the web.\\r\\n- **Ecosystem-wide** (\\"which libraries do X\\", \\"who else hit this\\") → no scope. Use `language` / `topic` / `min_stars` to keep to maintained repositories, accepting that this gives up all `doc` results.\\r\\n- **Agent skills and tooling conventions** → `skills=\\"only\\"` (HTTP/MCP only).\\r\\n- **Comparison, opinion, news, or an unindexed project** → the open web. `firecrawl_search`, then `firecrawl_scrape` whatever deserves a full read. Combining is often right: take the contract from the index and the trade-off from the web.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Principles\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quote the passage, cite the `url`.** The passages are the evidence; hand them over rather than paraphrasing them into a claim the reader can\'t check. `title` is frequently absent on `doc` results — fall back to `url`.\\r\\n- **A merge supersedes a report.** When an issue and a pull request disagree, the merged pull request is the current behaviour. Say which one you read.\\r\\n- **Scope last, not first.** Search the whole index, then narrow with `types`, `repos`, or `sources` once you know what the hits look like. Scoping first hides the result that would have told you where to look.\\r\\n- **Go to the web when the index has nothing to say.** Trade-offs, ecosystem opinion, and anything about an unindexed project are web questions. Don\'t force them through the index, and don\'t dress a general web page up as a primary source.\\r\\n\\r\\n## See also\\r\\n\\r\\n- [firecrawl-build-search](https://github.com/firecrawl/skills/tree/main/skills/build/firecrawl-build-search) — building the developer index into an app instead of querying it here\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE firecrawl-research-index \\r\\n ---\\r\\nname: firecrawl-research-index\\r\\ndescription: Find the papers that answer a research query in Firecrawl\'s research paper index — a corpus of paper abstracts whose largest share is biomedical and life-science literature (PubMed, bioRxiv, medRxiv), alongside arXiv preprints in CS, physics, and math — using semantic search, semantic and structural expansion, and in-body verification. Use this skill for literature-finding and paper-retrieval tasks of any kind, including clinical, biomedical, drug, gene, disease, and other life-science questions, whether the answer is a single paper or a full multi-paper set. The index is reached only through the `firecrawl_research_*` MCP tools or the `firecrawl research` CLI subcommands. Calling `firecrawl_search` with its `categories` option set to `[\\"research\\"]` is a different feature — it filters ordinary web search to research-affiliated websites (the list includes PubMed, bioRxiv, medRxiv, arXiv, and publisher sites) and returns page results from them, without querying the paper records in this index.\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Firecrawl Research Index\\r\\n\\r\\nFind the research papers that answer a research query. Some questions have a single answer; many have several — and when in doubt, lean toward returning the fuller relevant set (most relevant first) rather than narrowing to one. A reader is better served seeing the neighboring methods and papers than having them silently dropped.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What is in the index\\r\\n\\r\\nPaper abstracts, with full text reachable per paper. The largest share of the corpus is **biomedical and life-science** literature — **PubMed** journal articles plus **bioRxiv** and **medRxiv** preprints — so clinical, drug, gene, disease, epidemiology, and public-health questions are in scope. **arXiv** preprints cover computer science, physics, and mathematics. Coverage outside those sources is thinner: a paper that exists only behind a publisher paywall or in a niche venue may not be indexed, and the general web tools below are the fallback when it isn\'t.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere is **no fixed recipe**. Read the query, decide what kind it is, and choose the approach below. Some queries need a single search; others need heavy structural/semantic expansion. Don\'t run machinery a query doesn\'t call for.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The tools, and what each is uniquely good at\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_research_search_papers(query, k?)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl research search-papers <query> [--k <number>]`**\\r\\n  Semantic (HyDE) search over **abstracts**. The natural first move for almost any query.\\r\\n  If results look thin or all-alike, re-run with a different framing (sibling domain, rival method, dataset/benchmark name) rather than giving up.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_research_related_papers(seed_ids, intent, mode?, k?)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl research related-papers <seedIds...> --intent <intent> [--mode <similar|citers|references>] [--k <number>]`**\\r\\n  Semantic and structural expansion, ranked to your `intent`.\\r\\n  This reaches papers semantic search _cannot_, and it\'s how you turn one good hit into the rest of a set.\\r\\n  `mode=similar` → niche siblings; `citers` → who uses/builds on the seeds; `references` → what they build on / compare against.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_research_inspect_paper(id)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl research inspect-paper <id>`**\\r\\n  Canonical metadata for **one** paper: title, abstract, authors, categories, source ids, and dates.\\r\\n  Use it after `search_papers` or `related_papers` when you need the complete citation/metadata for a candidate, or when you have an id from elsewhere and need to confirm what paper it resolves to.\\r\\n  This does **not** read the paper body; use `read_paper` for specific full-text questions.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_research_read_paper(id, question)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl research read-paper <id> --question <question>`**\\r\\n  In-body passages of **one** paper, to verify a load-bearing constraint (a method actually used, a score actually reported, an affiliation, what a paper compares to).\\r\\n  Use it to settle a specific doubt, not on everything.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_search(query, categories: [\\"research\\"])`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl search <query> --categories research`**\\r\\n  **Not this index.** This is a _website_ filter: it restricts a normal web search to a short list of research-affiliated domains — the list does include `pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov`, `biorxiv.org`, `medrxiv.org`, and `arxiv.org` alongside publisher sites — and returns page results in a `research` group beside `web`, each with `url`, `title`, `description` (the matched passage), `position`, and `category: \\"research\\"` — web results carry no `category`, so that is the field to key on when merging.\\r\\n  So it reaches those sites\' **web pages**; what it does not do is query their **paper records** in this index — no semantic search over abstracts, no citation-graph or related-paper expansion, no canonical paper metadata, and no in-body passages. The results are ordinary web results.\\r\\n  Use it when you are **already** running a web search and want those sites weighed in the same call. For anything that is actually a paper-finding task, use `firecrawl_research_search_papers` and its siblings above.\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: **`firecrawl_search(query)` / `firecrawl_scrape(url)`**\\r\\n  CLI: **`firecrawl search <query>` / `firecrawl scrape <url>`**\\r\\n  General **web** search and page fetch, for facts that don\'t live in paper abstracts: benchmark **leaderboards**, rankings, \\"who scores best / is largest / is most used.\\"\\r\\n  Find the ranking on the web, then map the top entries back to papers with `search_papers`.\\r\\n  Reach for these only when the corpus can\'t answer the question on its own.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Match the approach to the query\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Single _named_ paper** (\\"the Qwen3 report\\") → one `search_papers`, done. This is the only case that truly wants exactly one paper.\\r\\n- **Paper by description / by method or technique** (\\"the paper that introduced X\\", \\"training-free N-gram detection of AI text\\") → find the best match, then assume there\'s a _family_: expand with `related_papers` and **include the closely-related methods/papers too**. Even when one paper is the exact literal match, surface and keep its neighbors — don\'t narrow to the single best hit and reason the rest out. Only treat it as one-answer if the query names a specific paper.\\r\\n- **Enumeration / method-family** (\\"papers that do X\\", \\"alternatives to Adam\\", \\"benchmarks for Y\\") → the answer is a _set_, and this is where `related_papers` earns its keep: expand several strong anchors with `mode=similar`, re-seed from new strong hits. One search is never enough here.\\r\\n- **Exhibiting** (\\"papers that _use_ / exhibit property P\\") → the relevant papers apply P but their abstracts may not describe it. Go from P\'s defining paper outward via `citers`/`references`, and use `read_paper` to confirm a candidate actually uses P.\\r\\n- **Superlative / leaderboard** (\\"best on benchmark X\\", \\"largest\\", \\"most popular\\") → the ranking lives on **leaderboards / the web**, not in any single abstract. Use `firecrawl_search` / `firecrawl_scrape` to find the benchmark\'s leaderboard or rankings, read off the top models/papers, then `search_papers` each to get its paper. As a fallback, search the benchmark and `read_paper` candidates for reported numbers. The hardest kind — cast wide.\\r\\n- **Org / author filtered** (\\"from \\\\<org\\\\>\\", \\"by \\\\<author\\\\>\\") → topical match isn\'t enough; verify the affiliation/authorship (metadata or `read_paper`) before keeping a paper.\\r\\n- **Compare-against** (\\"what does paper X benchmark against / build on\\") → the answer is _inside_ paper X: `read_paper(X, ...)` or `related_papers([X], ..., mode=\\"references\\")`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Principles\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Two different features share the word \\"research.\\"** The paper index is `firecrawl_research_*` / `firecrawl research`. The `categories: [\\"research\\"]` option on `firecrawl_search` is a website filter — it does point web search at PubMed, bioRxiv, medRxiv, arXiv, and publisher sites, but what comes back is their web pages, not paper records. If a task is about finding papers, the tools in this skill are the ones that read the corpus; reaching for `categories: [\\"research\\"]` will quietly answer a different question.\\r\\n- **Query shape and subject field are separate.** A clinical-trial question and a machine-learning question take the same shapes above; what differs is only which source the hits come from. Don\'t send a biomedical or life-science query to the open web on the assumption the corpus is arXiv-only — PubMed, bioRxiv, and medRxiv are the largest part of what `search_papers` reads.\\r\\n- **When in doubt, include.** For any topic / method / comparison question, return the relevant _family_, not just the single best match — err toward keeping a plausibly-relevant paper rather than dropping it. The neighboring methods are part of a good answer; don\'t reason close work out just because one paper is the most exact match.\\r\\n- **Follow the literature, and keep what you find.** The seminal source, the competing methods, the close neighbors are usually a hop away — use `related_papers`, and _include_ them, not just the first hit. Stopping at one good result is the most common way to leave the reader with half an answer.\\r\\n- **Verify to exclude, not to gatekeep.** Use `read_paper` to rule a paper _out_ when a hard constraint clearly fails (wrong org/author, doesn\'t actually report the score). When a paper is plausibly relevant, lean toward keeping it rather than demanding proof.\\r\\n- **Only drop the clearly off-topic.** Don\'t pad with papers you\'re confident are unrelated — but that\'s a high bar; most plausibly-relevant work should make the cut.\\r\\n\\r\\n## See also\\r\\n\\r\\n- [firecrawl-build-search](https://github.com/firecrawl/skills/tree/main/skills/build/firecrawl-build-search) — building the paper index into an app instead of querying it here\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

## Tool: exec

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"python -X utf8 -c \"import json,pathlib;d=json.load(open('audits/skills-2026-10-09/global-review.json',encoding='utf-8'));names=['firecrawl-research-papers','firecrawl-website-design-clone'];[(print('\\nFILE',n,'\\n',pathlib.Path(next(x['path'] for x in d['rows'] if x['name']==n)).read_text(encoding='utf-8-sig'))) for n in names]\"",max_output_tokens:6000}));


## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 4.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"3581fc","wall_time_seconds":0.5196161,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":3820,"output":"\\r\\nFILE firecrawl-research-papers \\r\\n ---\\r\\nname: firecrawl-research-papers\\r\\ndescription: Find and synthesize research papers, whitepapers, PDFs, technical reports, and academic sources with Firecrawl Research, using semantic paper search, related-paper expansion, and in-body verification over Firecrawl\'s paper index — largely biomedical and life-science literature from PubMed, bioRxiv, and medRxiv, plus arXiv preprints in CS, physics, and math. Use when the user wants a literature review, systematic review, survey of studies, paper summary, research landscape, or sourced synthesis from scholarly and industry publications, including clinical, drug, gene, disease, epidemiology, and public-health topics. Prefer this over a general web-research workflow whenever the evidence base is published papers rather than web pages.\\r\\nlicense: ISC\\r\\nmetadata:\\r\\n  author: firecrawl\\r\\n  version: \\"0.1.0\\"\\r\\n  homepage: https://www.firecrawl.dev\\r\\n  source: https://github.com/firecrawl/firecrawl-workflows\\r\\ninputs:\\r\\n  - name: FIRECRAWL_API_KEY\\r\\n    description: Firecrawl API key for hosted Firecrawl Research, CLI, MCP, or equivalent tool requests.\\r\\n    required: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Firecrawl Research Papers\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this to create a sourced literature review.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Onboarding Interview\\r\\n\\r\\nInfer the topic, source constraints, target count, and output format from context. If the topic is clear, proceed immediately.\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk at most 1-3 concise questions only if blocked, such as the topic, target paper count, or required venue/date/method constraints.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Firecrawl Collection Plan\\r\\n\\r\\nUse Firecrawl Research through the CLI, MCP, or equivalent Firecrawl tool\\r\\nsurface as the primary path for paper discovery and verification. Fall back to\\r\\ngeneral Firecrawl search and scrape for whitepapers, technical reports,\\r\\nresearch blogs, leaderboards, or facts outside the paper corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhat the paper index holds: paper abstracts, with full text reachable per\\r\\npaper. Its largest share is biomedical and life-science literature — PubMed\\r\\njournal articles plus bioRxiv and medRxiv preprints — so clinical, drug, gene,\\r\\ndisease, epidemiology, and public-health questions are in scope. arXiv\\r\\npreprints cover computer science, physics, and mathematics. Coverage outside\\r\\nthose sources is thinner, and the web tools below are the fallback there.\\r\\n\\r\\nCore tools:\\r\\n\\r\\n- MCP: `firecrawl_research_search_papers(query, k?)`\\r\\n  CLI: `firecrawl research search-papers <query> [--k <number>]`\\r\\n  Semantic search over paper abstracts. Start here for most paper-finding\\r\\n  queries, and retry with alternate framing when results are thin or too\\r\\n  narrow.\\r\\n- MCP: `firecrawl_research_related_papers(seed_ids, intent, mode?, k?)`\\r\\n  CLI: `firecrawl research related-papers <seedIds...> --intent <intent> [--mode <similar|citers|references>] [--k <number>]`\\r\\n  Expand from strong seed papers into similar work, citing papers, or\\r\\n  references. Use this to find the relevant paper family, not just the first\\r\\n  matching result.\\r\\n- MCP: `firecrawl_research_inspect_paper(id)`\\r\\n  CLI: `firecrawl research inspect-paper <id>`\\r\\n  Fetch canonical metadata for a candidate paper: title, abstract, authors,\\r\\n  categories, source ids, and dates.\\r\\n- MCP: `firecrawl_research_read_paper(id, question)`\\r\\n  CLI: `firecrawl research read-paper <id> --question <question>`\\r\\n  Verify a specific claim or constraint inside one paper, such as method,\\r\\n  reported score, benchmark, affiliation, comparison, or limitation.\\r\\n- MCP: `firecrawl_search(query)` / `firecrawl_scrape(url)`\\r\\n  CLI: `firecrawl search <query>` / `firecrawl scrape <url>`\\r\\n  Use for web-only context: benchmark leaderboards, rankings, reports,\\r\\n  whitepapers, research blogs, and source pages outside the paper index.\\r\\n\\r\\nNot the paper index, despite the name: passing `categories: [\\"research\\"]` to\\r\\n`firecrawl_search` (CLI `firecrawl search <query> --categories research`)\\r\\nfilters an ordinary web search to research-affiliated websites — the list\\r\\nincludes PubMed, bioRxiv, medRxiv, arXiv, and publisher sites — and returns\\r\\npage results from them. It reaches those sites\' web pages; what it does not do\\r\\nis query their paper records in the index above, so there is no abstract\\r\\nsearch, no related-paper or citation-graph expansion, no canonical paper\\r\\nmetadata, and no in-body passages. Use it when a web search is what you want\\r\\nand those sites should be weighted in the same call; use the\\r\\n`firecrawl_research_*` tools for paper work.\\r\\n\\r\\nMatch the approach to the query:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Single named paper: run one paper search, then inspect or read the paper if\\r\\n  metadata or body verification is needed.\\r\\n- Paper by description, method, or topic family: search for strong anchors,\\r\\n  then expand with related papers and keep close neighbors.\\r\\n- Enumeration queries, such as papers that do a task or benchmark a method:\\r\\n  search multiple framings, expand several strong anchors, and re-seed from\\r\\n  newly found relevant papers.\\r\\n- Papers that use or exhibit a property: start from the defining paper or\\r\\n  strongest anchor, expand via similar, citers, or references, and use\\r\\n  read-paper to verify the property.\\r\\n- Superlatives and leaderboards: use general web search or scrape to find the\\r\\n  ranking, then map top entries back to papers with paper search.\\r\\n- Author, organization, venue, date, or methodology constraints: verify with\\r\\n  inspect-paper metadata or read-paper before keeping a candidate.\\r\\n\\r\\nTarget source types:\\r\\n\\r\\n- biomedical and life-science literature from PubMed, with bioRxiv and medRxiv\\r\\n  preprints for work that has not appeared in a journal yet\\r\\n- arXiv preprints in computer science, physics, and mathematics\\r\\n- academic papers from university sites and ACM/IEEE pages where accessible\\r\\n- industry reports and whitepapers\\r\\n- company research blogs\\r\\n- technical articles and conference summaries\\r\\n\\r\\nPrinciples:\\r\\n\\r\\n- When in doubt, include the relevant paper family rather than only the single\\r\\n  best result.\\r\\n- Use related-paper expansion to avoid stopping at one strong hit.\\r\\n- Use read-paper to verify load-bearing constraints, not to summarize every\\r\\n  candidate.\\r\\n- Drop only clearly off-topic papers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Parallel Work\\r\\n\\r\\nIf appropriate, use sub-agents or equivalent parallel task runners:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Academic Papers researcher\\r\\n- Biomedical and Life Sciences researcher, for PubMed journal articles and\\r\\n  bioRxiv/medRxiv preprints on a clinical, drug, gene, disease, epidemiology,\\r\\n  or public-health topic\\r\\n- Industry Reports researcher\\r\\n- Technical Articles researcher\\r\\n- Synthesis and citation reviewer\\r\\n\\r\\nSplit by source or sub-topic, not by tool. Give each researcher the same paper\\r\\ntools and let the topic decide which part of the corpus answers.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Final Deliverable\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n# Literature Review: [Topic]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Abstract\\r\\n[2-3 paragraph summary]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Key Papers\\r\\n[Title, authors, source URL, key findings, methodology, relevance]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Themes And Consensus\\r\\n[What sources agree on]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Open Questions And Debates\\r\\n[Disagreements and unresolved questions]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Emerging Trends\\r\\n[Recent developments]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Sources\\r\\n[Organized by paper/report/article]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rerun Inputs\\r\\nworkflow: firecrawl-research-papers\\r\\ntopic: [topic]\\r\\ntarget_count: [number]\\r\\noutput: [markdown/brief]\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n## Quality Bar\\r\\n\\r\\n- Every major claim should trace to a source.\\r\\n- Note inaccessible or failed PDFs.\\r\\n- Distinguish peer-reviewed work from blogs and vendor reports.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nFILE firecrawl-website-design-clone \\r\\n ---\\r\\nname: firecrawl-website-design-clone\\r\\ndescription: Extract any website\'s design system into an agent-ready DESIGN.md using Firecrawl scrape evidence. Use when the user wants colors, fonts, spacing, components, layout patterns, or brand/UI guidance from a website so AI agents can create new websites, clone a look, or build pages inspired by that design.\\r\\nlicense: ISC\\r\\nmetadata:\\r\\n  author: firecrawl\\r\\n  version: \\"0.1.0\\"\\r\\n  homepage: https://www.firecrawl.dev\\r\\n  source: https://github.com/firecrawl/firecrawl-workflows\\r\\ninputs:\\r\\n  - name: FIRECRAWL_API_KEY\\r\\n    description: Firecrawl API key for hosted Firecrawl requests when the workflow runs through the CLI or API.\\r\\n    required: true\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Firecrawl Website Design Clone\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this when the user wants one URL turned into a practical design system file agents can use immediately.\\r\\n\\r\\nDefault outcome: **extract any website\'s design system in one line** and format it as `DESIGN.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe skill should feel like a thin workflow around Firecrawl scrape: gather the page\'s visible content, structure, metadata, links, and available visual signals, then synthesize those findings into a clean design-system markdown file.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Onboarding Interview\\r\\n\\r\\nInfer the source URL, target stack, and whether implementation is requested from context. If the user gives a URL and asks for a design system, proceed immediately.\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk at most 1-3 concise questions only if blocked, such as the website URL, whether to output only `DESIGN.md` or also implement, or a required target stack.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the host agent\'s normal prompt or modal UI. Do not name a harness-specific question function.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Firecrawl Collection Plan\\r\\n\\r\\nUse Firecrawl through the CLI or equivalent tool surface. Always start with two parallel scrapes of the supplied URL:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. The `branding` and `images` formats together for structured design tokens and the full set of page images.\\r\\n2. A full-page screenshot for visual context.\\r\\n\\r\\nExample:\\r\\n\\r\\n```bash\\r\\nfirecrawl scrape \\"https://example.com\\" --format branding,images -o \\".firecrawl/example-branding.json\\" --pretty &\\r\\nfirecrawl scrape \\"https://example.com\\" --full-page-screenshot -o \\".firecrawl/example-screenshot.png\\" &\\r\\nwait\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nCombining `branding` and `images` in one call still costs a single credit and is required: the `branding` block only surfaces curated brand assets (`logo`, `favicon`, `ogImage`, `logoHref`), so without `images` the agent will miss the page\'s actual content imagery (heroes, product shots, carousel slides, feature visuals, illustrations, accessory photos, end-of-page artwork, and similar). On a product page like `tesla.com/cybertruck` the `branding` block has no hero — only `images` returns the main Cybertruck hero (e.g. `Cybertruck-Hero-Desktop-NA-SA-APAC.png`) and the rest of the page\'s photography.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the screenshot scrape returns a remote image URL (e.g. signed storage link) instead of a local file, download it to the same `.firecrawl/` path so `DESIGN.md` can reference a stable local asset.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the structured `branding` output as the primary source for colors, typography, components, brand assets (logo, favicon, ogImage), personality, and confidence notes. Use the `images` list as the source of truth for the page\'s content imagery — hero photography, product shots, carousels, feature visuals, illustrations, and decorative graphics. Use the screenshot as the primary visual reference for layout, hierarchy, and overall feel. Add supplemental formats only when these are insufficient for the final artifact.\\r\\n\\r\\nCollect:\\r\\n\\r\\n- branding data for colors, typography, spacing, buttons, logos, brand imagery, personality, and confidence\\r\\n- the full `images` list for hero, product, feature, and section imagery beyond the curated brand assets\\r\\n- a full-page screenshot saved locally in `.firecrawl/` so it can be embedded in `DESIGN.md`\\r\\n- page markdown for headings, copy hierarchy, CTAs, navigation, and section order when needed\\r\\n- metadata and links for brand, product, and page-purpose clues when needed\\r\\n- HTML only when the branding output, images list, and screenshot are insufficient to infer classes, font names, CSS variables, or component structure\\r\\n- related pages only when the user asks for a broader site system\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not over-crawl by default. The first version should be useful from a single representative page.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What To Extract\\r\\n\\r\\nInfer and document the site\'s design language:\\r\\n\\r\\n- colors: primary, secondary, accents, backgrounds, borders, text, states\\r\\n- typography: font families if detectable, type scale, weights, line heights, heading/body treatment\\r\\n- spacing: container widths, section rhythm, grid gaps, padding scale, density\\r\\n- layout: page structure, hero patterns, cards, grids, nav, footer, responsive assumptions\\r\\n- components: buttons, inputs, cards, badges, nav items, pricing blocks, testimonials, feature rows, forms\\r\\n- imagery and icons: style, shape language, illustration/photo treatment, logo constraints; pull representative hero, product, feature, and section images from the full `images` list rather than relying on `branding.images`, which only carries `logo`, `favicon`, `ogImage`, and `logoHref`\\r\\n- motion and interaction: hover states, transitions, animation style when observable or inferable\\r\\n- voice and content patterns: CTA wording, heading style, product copy rhythm\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen a value cannot be measured exactly from scrape output, label it as inferred and give a practical approximation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Parallel Work\\r\\n\\r\\nIf appropriate, use sub-agents or equivalent parallel task runners. Natural splits include one page per researcher for multi-page sites, or one reviewer each for colors, typography, spacing, and components.\\r\\n\\r\\nEach parallel researcher should return source URLs, extracted evidence, inferred design tokens, and confidence notes.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Final Deliverable\\r\\n\\r\\nCreate or return a `DESIGN.md` with this structure. Embed the full-page screenshot near the top so a coding agent gets visual context alongside the tokens.\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n# DESIGN.md: [Source Site]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source\\r\\n- URL: [source URL]\\r\\n- Capture date: [date]\\r\\n- Evidence: [scrape/screenshot/html/links used]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reference Screenshot\\r\\n![Full-page screenshot of [Source Site]](./.firecrawl/[source]-screenshot.png)\\r\\n\\r\\nUse this screenshot as the visual source of truth for layout, hierarchy, density, and feel. Tokens below describe the same page in machine-readable form.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Design Summary\\r\\n[Short description of the visual language and what an agent should recreate]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Design Tokens\\r\\n\\r\\n### Colors\\r\\n[Named color roles with hex values when known; mark inferred values clearly]\\r\\n\\r\\n### Typography\\r\\n[Fonts, fallback recommendations, scale, weights, heading/body rules]\\r\\n\\r\\n### Spacing And Layout\\r\\n[Spacing scale, containers, grids, radius, shadows, borders]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Components\\r\\n[Buttons, cards, nav, forms, hero, feature sections, pricing, footer, etc.]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Page Patterns\\r\\n[Section order, common layouts, responsive behavior]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Content Style\\r\\n[Voice, CTA style, heading patterns, copy density]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Agent Build Instructions\\r\\n[Concrete instructions an AI coding agent can follow to create a new site in this style]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Rerun Inputs\\r\\nworkflow: firecrawl-website-design-clone\\r\\nsource_url: [url]\\r\\ntarget_stack: [stack]\\r\\noutput: DESIGN.md\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the user asks to implement, first produce or update `DESIGN.md`, then use it as the source of truth for the build.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Quality Bar\\r\\n\\r\\n- Do not imply the user has rights to third-party logos, images, trademarks, or copy.\\r\\n- Prefer reusable design tokens over one-off observations.\\r\\n- Distinguish observed facts from inferred approximations.\\r\\n- Keep the output compact enough that another agent can paste it into context and build from it.\\r\\n- Preserve source URLs and scrape artifacts for review.\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]
